Then,  $\hat{r}_k = r_k, \forall k \in [d]$  with probability at least  $1 - Ce^{-c\bar{p}}$ .

In practice, we can also apply a simple criterion of the cumulative percentage of total variation (Jolliffe, 1986, Chapter 6.1.1) originating from principle component analysis:

$$
\hat {r} _ {k} = \arg \min  \left\{r: \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {r} \sigma_ {i} ^ {2} \left(\mathcal {M} _ {k} (\tilde {\mathcal {X}})\right) / \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {p _ {k}} \sigma_ {i} ^ {2} \left(\mathcal {M} _ {k} (\tilde {\mathcal {X}})\right) \geq \rho \right\} \tag {5.3}
$$

Here,  $\rho \in (0,1)$  is some empirical thresholding level. We will illustrate this principle on real data analysis in Section 7.2. Under the general deterministic setting, the accurate (or optimal) estimation of tensor rank may be much more challenging and we leave it as future work.

6. Extensions to General Order- $d$  Tensors. While our previous sections mainly focus on order-3 tensor estimation, our results can be generalized to the order- $d$  low-rank tensor estimation with the key ideas outlined in this section. First, the constraint set  $\mathcal{C}$ , RCG condition and noise quantity  $\xi$  can be defined similarly by replacing the order-3 tensor with the general low-rank order- $d$  tensors; second, the local convergence analysis can be similarly conducted as Theorem 3.1. Define

$$
E^{(t)}:= \min_{\substack{\mathbf{R}_{k}\in \mathbb{O}_{p_{k},r_{k}}\\ k = 1,\ldots ,d}}\left\{\sum_{k = 1}^{d}\left\| \mathbf{U}_{k}^{(t)} - \mathbf{U}_{k}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2} + \left\| \mathcal{S}^{(t)} - \llbracket \mathcal{S}^{*};\mathbf{R}_{1}^{\top},\ldots ,\mathbf{R}_{d}^{\top}\rrbracket \right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\right\} .
$$

We can build the equivalence between  $E^{(t)}$  and

$$
\left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {a}{2} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {d} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}
$$

by setting  $b \asymp \overline{\lambda}^{1/(d+1)}$  and  $a \asymp \overline{\lambda}$ . Then, we can establish the following theoretical guarantee under a good initialization:

Theorem 6.1 (Informal) Suppose  $L$  satisfies  $RCG(\alpha, \beta, \mathcal{C})$  and assume  $\kappa, \alpha, \beta$  are constants. Assume  $\mathcal{X}^* = [[\mathcal{S}^*; \mathbf{U}_1^*, \ldots, \mathbf{U}_d^*]]$  and the initialization  $\mathcal{X}^{(0)} = [[\mathcal{S}^{(0)}; \mathbf{U}_1^{(0)}, \ldots, \mathbf{U}_d^{(0)}]]$  satisfy

$$
\mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {* \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} = \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(0) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(0)} = b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}}, \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*}, \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(0)} \in \mathcal {C} _ {k}, k = 1, \dots , d; \mathcal {S} ^ {*}, \mathcal {S} ^ {(k)} \in \mathcal {C} _ {\mathcal {S}}. \tag {6.1}
$$

Suppose the initialization error satisfies  $\left\| \mathcal{X}^{(0)} - \mathcal{X}^{*}\right\| \leq c_{d}\underline{\lambda}^{2}$  and the signal-noise-ratio satisfies  $\underline{\lambda}^2\geq C_d\xi^2$ . Then, by taking step size  $\eta \leq c_{d}b^{-2d}$ , the output of Algorithm 3.1 satisfies

$$
\left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq C _ {d} ^ {\prime} \left(\xi^ {2} + \left(1 - c _ {d} ^ {\prime} \eta\right) ^ {t} \left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(0)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right).
$$

Here,  $C_d, c_d, C_d', c_d'$  are constants only depending on  $d$ .

It is worth mentioning that Theorem 6.1 applies to low-rank matrix estimation (i.e.  $d = 2$ ): suppose  $\mathbf{X}^{*} = \mathbf{U}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{S}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{2}^{*^{\top}}$  and  $\mathbf{X}^{(0)} = \mathbf{U}_1^{(0)}\mathbf{S}^{(0)}\mathbf{U}_2^{(0)^{\top}}$  for  $\mathbf{U}_k,\mathbf{U}_k^{(0)}\in \mathbb{R}^{p_k\times r}$ ,  $\mathbf{S}^{*},\mathbf{S}^{(0)}\in \mathbb{R}^{r\times r}$ , we have

$$
\left\| \mathbf {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq C \left(\xi^ {2} + (1 - c \eta) ^ {t} \left\| \mathbf {X} ^ {(0)} - \mathbf {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right).
$$

<table><tr><td>Application</td><td>SNR condition</td><td>Estimation error</td><td>Lower bound</td></tr><tr><td>sub-Gaussian tensor-PCA</td><td>\underline{\lambda}/\sigma \gtrsim p^{d/4}r^{1/(d+1)}</td><td>\(\sigma^2\left(pr + r^d\right)\)</td><td>\(\sigma^2\left(pr + r^d\right)\)</td></tr><tr><td>Tensor regression</td><td>n \gtrsim p^{d/2}r</td><td>n^{-1}\(\sigma^2\left(pr + r^d\right)\)</td><td>n^{-1}\(\sigma^2\left(pr + r^d\right)\)</td></tr><tr><td>Poisson tensor-PCA</td><td>\(\sqrt{I\underline{\lambda}} \gtrsim p^{(d-1)/2}r^{1/2}\)</td><td>I^{-1}\(\left(pr + r^d\right)\)</td><td>I^{-1}\(\left(pr + r^d\right)\)</td></tr><tr><td>Binomial tensor-PCA</td><td>\(\sqrt{N\underline{\lambda}} \gtrsim p^{(d-1)/2}r^{1/2}\)</td><td>N^{-1}\(\left(pr + r^d\right)\)</td><td>N^{-1}\(\left(pr + r^d\right)\)</td></tr></table>

TABLE 2

Minimax optimal estimation error bounds for order-  $d$  low-rank tensor estimation in specific applications

$$
(d \geq 2). H e r e, f o r s i m p l i c i t y, r _ {1} = r _ {2} = r _ {3} = r, p _ {1} = p _ {2} = p _ {3} = p a n d r \leq p ^ {1 / 2}.
$$

While this framework is more complicated than necessary since one can always decompose a low-rank matrix as the product of two factor matrices  $\mathbf{X} = \mathbf{U}_1\mathbf{U}_2^\top$  without explicitly introducing the "core matrix"  $\mathbf{S} \in \mathbb{R}^{r \times r}$  (see our previous discussions in Section 3.3).

Based on Theorem 6.1, we can further extend the minimax optimal bounds for the proposed procedure in each application of Section 4, i.e., Theorems 4.1-4.6 to high-order scenarios. We summarize the results to Table 2.

# 7. Numerical Studies.

7.1. Synthetic Data Analysis. In this section, we investigate the numerical performance of the proposed methods on the problems discussed in Section 4 with simulated data. We assume the true rank  $(r_1,r_2,r_3)$  is known to us and the algorithm only involves two tuning parameters:  $a$  and  $b$ . According to Theorem 3.1, a proper choice of  $a$  and  $b$  primarily depends on the unknown value  $\overline{\lambda}$ . In practice, we propose to use the initial estimate  $\mathcal{X}^{(0)}$  as an approximation of  $\mathcal{X}^*$ , use  $\bar{\lambda}^{(0)} = \max_k\left\| \mathcal{M}_k(\mathcal{X}^{(0)})\right\|$  as a plug-in estimate of  $\overline{\lambda}$ , then choose  $a = \overline{\lambda}^{(0)}, b = (\overline{\lambda}^{(0)})^{1 / 4}$ . We consider the following root mean squared error (RMSE) to assess the estimation accuracy in all settings:

$$
\operatorname {L o s s} \left(\hat {\mathcal {X}}, \mathcal {X} ^ {*}\right) = \left(p _ {1} p _ {2} p _ {3}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \| \hat {\mathcal {X}} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \| _ {\mathrm {F}}. \tag {7.1}
$$

Average loss over 100 repetitions are reported in following different scenarios.

Tensor Regression. We investigate the numerical performance of the proposed procedure in low-rank tensor regression discussed in Section 4.2. For all simulation settings, we first generate an  $r_1$ -by- $r_2$ -by- $r_3$  core tensor  $\bar{S}$  with i.i.d. standard Gaussian entries and rescale it as  $\mathcal{S} = \bar{\mathcal{S}} \cdot \lambda / \min_{k=1}^{3} \sigma_r(\mathcal{M}_k(\bar{\mathcal{S}}))$ . Here,  $\lambda$  quantifies the signal level and will be specified later. Then we generate  $\mathbf{U}_k$  uniformly at random from the Stiefel manifold  $\mathbb{O}_{p_k, r_k}$  and calculate the true parameter as  $\mathcal{X}^* = [[\mathcal{S}; \mathbf{U}_1, \mathbf{U}_2, \mathbf{U}_3]]$ . The rescaling procedure here ensures that  $\min_k \sigma_{r_k}(\mathcal{M}_k(\mathcal{X}^*)) \geq \lambda$ . Now, we draw a random sample based on the regression model (4.2).

We aim to compare the proposed method (Algorithms 1 and 3) with the initialization estimator (Algorithm 3 solely), Tucker-Regression method $^2$ , and MLE. Since the MLE corresponds to the global minimum of the rank-constrained optimization (2.2) and is often computationally intractable, we instead consider a warm-start gradient descent estimator, i.e., performing Algorithm 1 starting from the true parameter  $\mathcal{X}^*$ . We expect that the output of this procedure can well approximate MLE. We implement all four procedures under two settings: (a)  $p_1 = p_2 = p_3 = p = 30$ ,  $r_1 = r_2 = r_3 = r = 5$ ,  $\lambda = 2$ ,  $\sigma = 1$ ,  $n$  varies from 300 to 1000; and (b)  $p$  varies from 20 to 50,  $r = 3$ ,  $\lambda = 2$ ,  $\sigma = 1$ ,  $n = 1.2p^{3/2}r$ . The results

![](images/6364e844b6bb7391851d8c845424f2374d81e38e803e9ef203f1679dbb39e472.jpg)  
FIG 1. Average estimation errors in low-rank tensor regression. The MLE (oracle) is approximated by running gradient descent with the initialization chosen at  $\mathcal{X}^*$ , which would not be useable in practice. Here,  $r = 3$ ,  $\lambda = 2$ ,  $\sigma = 1$ . Left panel:  $p = 30$ ,  $n \in [300, 1000]$ . Right panel:  $p \in [25, 50]$ ,  $n = 1.2p^{3/2}r$ .

![](images/822a901d9729d44fa7be0346a7bf9d3b390f3970552879e9f357bd80195605a1.jpg)

are collected in Figure 1. We see from the left panel that for small sample size ( $n \leq 600$ ), the proposed gradient descent method significantly outperforms the Tucker-Regression and initialization estimator while has larger estimation errors than MLE. When the sample size increases ( $n \geq 700$ ), the performance of the proposed gradient descent and Tucker regression algorithms tend to be as good as MLE. Compared to the initialization, gradient descent achieves a great improvement on the estimation accuracy. The right panel of Figure 1 shows that the gradient descent performs as good as the warm-start gradient descent asymptotically and is significantly better than the initialization and Tucker-Regression estimators.

Poisson Tensor PCA. Next, we study the numerical performance of the proposed procedure on Poisson tensor PCA. As mentioned earlier in Section 4.3, we found that the projection steps in Algorithm 1 are not essential to the numerical performance, thus we apply Algorithm 1 without the projection steps there in all numerical experiments for Poisson tensor PCA.

For each experiment, we first generate a random core tensor  $S \in \mathbb{R}^{r_1 \times r_2 \times r_3}$  with i.i.d. standard normal entries and random orthogonal matrices  $\mathbf{U}_k$  uniformly on Stiefel manifold  $\mathbb{O}^{p_k \times r_k}$ . Then we calculate  $\tilde{\mathcal{X}} = S \times_1 \mathbf{U}_1 \times_2 \mathbf{U}_2 \times_3 \mathbf{U}_3$  and rescale it as  $\mathcal{X}^* = \bar{\mathcal{X}} \cdot B / \left\| \tilde{\mathcal{X}} \right\|_{\infty}$  to ensure that each entry of  $\mathcal{X}^*$  is bounded by  $B$ . Now, we generate a count tensor  $\mathcal{Y} \in \mathbb{N}^{p_1 \times p_2 \times p_3}$ :  $\mathcal{Y}_{jkl} \sim \mathrm{Poisson}(I \exp(\mathcal{X}_{jkl}^*))$  independently, and aim to estimate the low-rank tensor  $\mathcal{X}^*$  based on  $\mathcal{Y}$ . In addition to the proposed method, we also consider the baseline methods of Poisson-HOSVD and Poisson-HOOI that perform HOSVD and HOOI on  $\log((\mathcal{Y} + 1/2)/I)$  (i.e., Algorithm 4).

First, we fix  $p = 50$ ,  $r = 5$ , vary the intensity value  $I$ , and study the effect of  $I$  to the numerical performance. As we can see from Figure 2, for low intensity, the gradient method is significantly better than two baselines (left panel); for high intensity, three methods are comparable while the Poisson gradient descent is the best (right panel). Next, we study their performance for different tensor dimensions and ranks. In the left panel of Figure 3, we set  $r = 5$  and vary  $p$  from 30 to 100; in the right panel of Figure 3, we fix  $p = 50$  and vary  $r$  from 5 to 15. As one can see, our method significantly outperforms the baselines in all settings. All these simulation results illustrate the benefits of applying gradient descent on the Poisson likelihood function.

Binomial Tensor PCA. We generate  $\mathcal{X}^*$  in the same way as the Poisson tensor PCA settings. Suppose we observe  $\mathcal{Y}\in \mathbb{N}^{p_1\times p_2\times p_3}$  generated from

$$
\mathcal {Y} _ {j k l} \sim \text {B i n o m i a l} \left(\mathcal {N} _ {j k l}, s \left(\mathcal {X} _ {j k l} ^ {*}\right)\right), \quad \text {i n d e p e n d e n t l y}.
$$

![](images/9d32c485ed0ff17d592e38aa886e1d9497c9a629cb4c9d336afba224b0d38db6.jpg)  
FIG 2. Average estimation error of Poisson tensor PCA. Here  $p = 50$ ,  $r = 5$ ,  $B = 2$ . Left panel: intensity parameter  $I \in [.5, 2]$ . Right panel:  $I \in [2, 20]$ .

![](images/9c7aaa4b5e73b17ed059b630cd1d7f59e210d0aafd51007c21b96bdda5e35b32.jpg)  
FIG 3. Average estimation error of Poisson tensor PCA with different dimensions and ranks. Here,  $B = 2$ ,  $I = 1$ . Left panel:  $r = 5$ ,  $p \in [30,300]$ . Right panel:  $p = 50$ ,  $r \in [5,15]$ .

![](images/4c3e6f3bdbe3d67fcecd20cb0bcd10461f46c0a86026ffe873800f2aaa8a8164.jpg)

We take all entries with the same population size (i.e.,  $\mathcal{N}_{ijk} = N$ ) for simplification. We can see from the simulation results in Figure 4 that a larger population size  $N$  yields smaller estimation error. In addition, according to Theorem 3.1, the estimation error in theory is of order  $O(p^{-1/2}r^{1/2})$ , which matches the trend of estimation error curves in Figure 4.

7.2. Real Data Analysis. In this section, we apply the proposed framework to real data applications in 4D-STEM image denoising. An additional real data example on click-through prediction is postponed to Appendix B in the supplementary materials.

The 4D-scanning transmission electron microscopy (4D-STEM) is an important technique in modern material science that has been used to detect local material composition of structures such as films, defects and nanostructures (Krivanek, Dellby and Lupini, 1999; Yankovich et al., 2016). In 4D-STEM imaging technology, a focused probe is usually rastered across part of the specimen and an X-ray and/or electron energy loss spectrum is recorded at each probe position, generating a series of photon-limited images. The data generated from 4D-STEM technique are typically order-4 tensors with approximate periodic structures, as a focused probe is located on a 2-D grid and one 2-D image is generated for each probe position (see Yankovich et al. (2016) for more details). Due to the physical conditions, the observable images are often photon-limited, highly noisy, and in the form of count matrices

![](images/fde1900901c1fa2a6d8de8f3ea91a99af0e3542b46239a7918d2027a719de268.jpg)  
FIG 4. Average estimation error of binomial tensor PCA. Here,  $B = 2$ . Left panel:  $r = 5$ ,  $p \in [30,100]$ . Right panel:  $p = 50$ ,  $r \in [5:15]$ .

![](images/4f63ee1259d17f3c047c922fcffd5392b87f4a0d9ddd5fedcf634c22786be1a0.jpg)

(see the second row of Figure 5 for an example). A sufficient imaging denoising is often a crucial first step before the subsequent procedures.

We aim to illustrate the merit of the proposed method through denoising of data in 4D-STEM experiments. Specifically, we collect 160 images generated from a row of electron probe positions<sup>3</sup>. Since the resolution of each image is  $183 \times 183$ , the data images can be stacked into a non-negative tensor of size  $160 \times 183 \times 183$ . We assume the observational images  $\mathcal{V}$  are generated from Poisson distribution  $\mathcal{Y}_{ijk} \stackrel{iid}{\sim} \mathrm{Poisson}(\exp(\mathcal{X}_{ijk}^{*}))$ . Our goal is to recover the original images based on the photon-limited observation  $\mathcal{V}$ . Since  $\mathcal{V}$  is sparse ( $\approx 88\%$  pixels are zero), we take the pre-initializer  $\tilde{\mathcal{X}} = \log(\mathcal{Y} + 1/30)$  and take the input rank according to the (5.3) with  $\rho = 0.98$ . We apply the proposed gradient descent (Algorithms 1 and 4) with the rank estimation  $(\hat{r}_1, \hat{r}_2, \hat{r}_3) = (44, 36, 34)$  to obtain the estimator  $\hat{\mathcal{X}}$ , then calculate  $\exp(\hat{\mathcal{X}})$  as the collection of denoised images. We also denoise these images one by one via the matrix Procrustes flow (Park et al., 2018), a variant of the matrix-version gradient descent method.<sup>4</sup> The original, observational, and recovered images are provided in Figure 5. In addition, we calculate the recovery loss for each of the 160 images, i.e.,  $\| \exp(\mathcal{X}_{i::}^{*}) - \exp(\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{i::}) \|_{\mathrm{F}} / \| \exp(\mathcal{X}_{i::}^{*}) \|_{\mathrm{F}}$ , and the averaged recovery loss (and standard error) of matrix and tensor methods are 0.861 (0.183) and 0.303 (0.054), respectively. One can clearly see the advantage of the proposed tensor method that utilizes the tensor structure of the whole set of images.

8. Discussions. In this paper, we introduce a non-convex optimization framework for the generalized tensor estimation. Compared to the convex relaxation methods in the literature, the proposed scheme is computationally efficient and achieves desirable statistical error rate under suitable initialization and signal-to-noise ratio conditions. We apply the proposed framework on several problems, including sub-Gaussian denoising, tensor regression, Poisson and binomial tensor PCA. We can show that the proposed gradient descent procedure achieves the minimax optimal rate of estimation error under these statistical models.

![](images/5ec56e019b3efe7a62a032feff06d4562115e588d78cc7837035bf67fdd782e7.jpg)

![](images/24fc3a5551bb2d8ffae703bac7014fad109225befb615ce2f1011b6c2a875fe2.jpg)

![](images/6788a76302eb9109023c075eba96252d1d39e96eed0ae7c6a1d908a23c16b278.jpg)

![](images/f45edd2f446b6c456eb993be9625c99c2a9de5d4b6d01e999c4bb2a2918321d3.jpg)  
FIG 5. Recovery results for the first five images of 4D-STEM data. First row: original images; second row: photon-limited observations; third row: denoised images by matrix method; forth row: denoised images by the proposed tensor method

In addition to the above-mentioned problems, the proposed framework can incorporate a broader range of settings. For example, the developed result is applicable to solve the noisy tensor completion problem (Xia, Yuan and Zhang, 2017; Montanari and Sun, 2018; Shah and Yu, 2019; Cai et al., 2019), which aims to recover the low-rank tensor  $\mathcal{X}^*$  based on a number of noisy observable entries, say  $\{\mathcal{Y}_{ijk} = \mathcal{X}_{ijk}^* +\mathcal{Z}_{ijk}\}_{(i,j,k)\in \Omega}$ , where  $\Omega$  is a subset of indices.

Another example is binary tensor PCA (Wang and Li, 2018), where the central goal is to factorize the tensor from 0-1 valued observations. Suppose one observes  $\mathcal{Y}_{ijk} \sim$  Bernoulli  $(\mathcal{P}_{ijk})$  independently, where  $\mathcal{P}_{ijk} = s(\mathcal{X}_{ijk}^*)$ ,  $\mathcal{X}^*$  is low-rank, and  $s(\cdot)$  is some link function. Then the proposed projected gradient descent method can be applied to estimate  $\mathcal{X}^*$  with provable guarantees.

Community detection in social network has attracted enormous recent attention. Although most of the existing results focused on a single-layer of network, the multilayer network, i.e., the connections between different nodes are reflected in multiple modalities, also commonly appear in practice (Han, Xu and Airoldi, 2015; Lei, Chen and Lynch, 2019; Pensky et al., 2019). Consider a stack of multilayer network data with shared community structure. It is reasonable to assume that the adjacency tensor  $\mathcal{A}$  has a low-rank tensor structure:

$\mathcal{A} \sim$  Bernoulli  $(\mathcal{X}^{*})$  independently, where  $\mathcal{X}^{*} = [[S^{*}, Z^{*}, Z^{*}, T^{*}]], Z^{*}$  is the latent space of nodes features (or the indicator matrix for the community that each node belongs to), and  $T^{*}$  models the trend along the time. Then the community detection for multilayer networks essentially becomes the generalized tensor estimation problem.

In addition to the standard linear regression model discussed in Section 4.2, the proposed framework can be applied to a range of generalized tensor regression problems. Recall that the classical generalized linear model focuses on an exponential family, where the response  $y_{i}$  satisfies the following density or probability mass function (Nelder and Wedderburn, 1972),

$$
p \left(y _ {i} \mid \theta_ {i}, \phi\right) = \exp \left\{\frac {y \theta_ {i} - b \left(\theta_ {i}\right)}{a (\phi)} + c (y, \phi) \right\}. \tag {8.1}
$$

Here,  $a,b,c$  are prespecified functions determined by the problem;  $\theta_{i}$  and  $\phi >0$  are natural and dispersion parameters, respectively. For the generalized tensor regression, it is natural to relate the tensor covariate and response (Zhou, Li and Zhu, 2013) via

$$
\mu_ {i} = \mathbb {E} \left(y _ {i} \mid \mathcal {X} ^ {*}\right) \quad g \left(\mu_ {i}\right) = \langle \mathcal {A} _ {i}, \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \rangle , \tag {8.2}
$$

where  $g(\cdot)$  is a link function. To estimate  $\mathcal{X}^*$ , we can apply the proposed Algorithm 3 on the negative log-likelihood function

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \frac {y _ {i} \theta_ {i} - b (\theta_ {i})}{a (\phi)} + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} c (y _ {i}, \phi),
$$

where  $\theta_{i}$  is determined by (8.1) and (8.2).

Some other possible applications of the proposed framework include the high-order interaction pursuit (Hao, Zhang and Cheng, 2019), generalized regression among multiple modes (Xu, Hu and Wang, 2019), mixed-data-type tensor data analysis (Baker, Tang and Allen, 2019), etc. In all these problems, by exploring the log-likelihood of data and the domain  $\mathcal{C}$  that satisfies RCG condition, the proposed projected gradient descent can be applied and the theoretical guarantees can be developed based on the proposed framework.

ZHOU, H., LI, L. and ZHU, H. (2013). Tensor regression with applications in neuroimaging data analysis. Journal of the American Statistical Association 108 540-552.  
ZHU, Z., LI, Q., TANG, G. and WAKIN, M. B. (2017). The global optimization geometry of nonsymmetric matrix factorization and sensing. arXiv preprint arXiv:1703.01256.

# Supplement to "An Optimal Statistical and Computational Framework for Generalized Tensor Estimation"

Rungang Han, Rebecca Willett, and Anru R. Zhang

In this supplement, we provide the implementation details of HeteroPCA, higher-order orthogonal iteration (HOOI), higher-order SVD (HOSVD), additional real data example, proofs of all the technical results, and the key technical lemmas.

# APPENDIX A: IMPLEMENTATION OF ADDITIONAL ALGORITHMS

We collect the implementations of HeteroPCA (Zhang, Cai and Wu, 2018), higher-order SVD (HOSVD), and higher-order orthogonal iteration (HOOI) (De Lathauwer, De Moor and Vandewalle, 2000b; Anandkumar, Hsu and Kakade, 2012) in this section. For any square matrix  $\mathbf{A}$ , let  $\Delta(\mathbf{A})$  be  $\mathbf{A}$  with all diagonal entries set to zero and  $D(\mathbf{A})$  be  $\mathbf{A}$  with all off-diagonal entries set to zero.

Algorithm 6 Heteroskedastic PCA (HeteroPCA)  
```latex
Require: symmetric matrix  $\hat{\Sigma}$ , rank  $r$ , max iteration time  $t_{max}$   
Set  $\mathbf{N}^{(0)} = \Delta (\hat{\Sigma})$ .  
for all  $t = 1, \ldots, t_{max}$  do  
Calculate SVD:  $\mathbf{N}^{(t)} = \sum_{i} \lambda_i u_i^{(t)} (v_i^{(t)})^\top$ , where  $\lambda_1 \geq \lambda_2 \geq \ldots \geq 0$ . Let  $\tilde{\mathbf{N}}^{(t)} = \sum_{i=1}^{r} \lambda_i^{(t)} u_i^{(t)} (v_i^{(t)})^\top$ . Update diagonal entries  $\mathbf{N}^{(t+1)} = D(\tilde{\mathbf{N}}^{(t)}) + \Delta (\mathbf{N}^{(t)})$ .  
end for  
return  $\mathbf{U} = (u_1^{(t_{max})}, \ldots, u_r^{(t_{max})})$
```

Algorithm 7 Higher-order Singular Value Decomposition (HOSVD)  
```txt
Require:  $\mathcal{V}\in \mathbb{R}^{p_1\times p_2\times p_3}$  , Tucker rank  $(r_1,r_2,r_3)$ $\mathbf{U}_k = \mathrm{SVD}_{r_k}$ $(\mathcal{M}_k(\mathcal{V}))$  for  $k = 1,2,3$ $\mathcal{S} = [[\mathcal{V};\mathbf{U}_1^\top ,\mathbf{U}_2^\top ,\mathbf{U}_3^\top ]]$  return  $(S,\mathbf{U}_1,\mathbf{U}_2,\mathbf{U}_3)$
```

Algorithm 8 Higher-order Orthogonal Iteration (HOOI)  
Require:  $\mathcal{V}\in \mathbb{R}^{p_1\times p_2\times p_3}$  , Tucker rank  $(r_1,r_2,r_3)$  , max iteration  $t_{max}$  Initialize  $\mathbf{U}_k^{(0)} = \mathrm{SVD}_{r_k}$ $(\mathcal{M}_k(\mathcal{Y}))$  for  $k = 1,2,3$    
for all  $t = 1,\dots t_{max}$  do   
for all  $k = 1,2,3$  do   
 $\mathbf{A}_k^{(t)} = \mathcal{M}_k\left(\mathcal{V}\times_{k + 1}\mathbf{U}_{k + 1}^{(t - 1)\top}\times_{k + 2}\mathbf{U}_{k + 2}^{(t - 1)\top}\right)$ $\mathbf{U}_k^{(t)} = \mathrm{SVD}_{r_k}\left(\mathbf{A}_k^{(t)}\right)$    
end for   
 $t = t + 1$    
end for   
return  $\left(S^{(t_{max})},\mathbf{U}_1^{(t_{max})},\mathbf{U}_2^{(t_{max})},\mathbf{U}_3^{(t_{max})}\right)$

<table><tr><td>Methods</td><td>Training Error</td><td>Testing Error</td></tr><tr><td>HOSVD</td><td>4.43(0.18)</td><td>5.34(0.24)</td></tr><tr><td>HOOI</td><td>4.31(0.15)</td><td>5.33(0.25)</td></tr><tr><td>Poisson-PCA</td><td>4.91(0.17)</td><td>5.27(0.22)</td></tr></table>

TABLE 3

Average training and prediction errors of click-through counts. The standard error of prediction errors is provided in the parentheses. After numerical explorations, we set the Tucker-rank to (5, 5, 2) in these experiments.

# APPENDIX B: ADDITIONAL REAL DATA EXAMPLE

We study the prediction of users' online click-through behavior on Taobao.com, one of the most popular online shopping website in China. The data<sup>5</sup> are collected from Nov 25 to Dec 02, 2017 and arranged into 32 periods as each day is divided into four periods: 00:00 – 06:00, 06:00 – 12:00, 12:00 – 18:00, and 18:00 – 24:00. By this means, the dataset is in the form of a count-valued third-order tensor, where the  $(i,j,k)$ th entry represents the total count of clicks by the  $i$ th user on  $j$ th item-category in the  $k$ th period along eight days. Due to the high-dimensionality of the original dataset ( $\approx 10^{6}$  users and  $\approx 10^{4}$  item categories), we only focus on the most active 100 users and the most popular 50 categories.

To investigate the predictive power of the proposed procedure, we consider all  $\binom{8}{4}=70$  even partitions of the eight days:  $\pi \cup \pi^c = \{1,\dots ,8\}$ ,  $\pi \cap \pi = \emptyset$ . For each  $\pi$ , we aggregate the dataset into two tensors  $\mathcal{Y}_1^\pi, \mathcal{Y}_2^\pi \in \mathbb{N}^{100\times 50\times 4}$ , where  $(\mathcal{Y}_1^\pi)_{ijk}$  and  $(\mathcal{Y}_2^\pi)_{ijk}$  are the sums of count clicks made by the  $i$ th customer on the  $j$ th item-category in the  $k$ th daily time interval on the days of  $\pi$  and  $\pi^c$ , respectively. We apply the proposed method (Algorithms 1 and 4 in Poisson tensor PCA), HOSVD (De Lathauwer, De Moor and Vandewalle, 2000b), and HOOI (De Lathauwer, De Moor and Vandewalle, 2000a) respectively on  $\mathcal{Y}_1^\pi$  to obtain the tensor estimator  $\hat{\mathcal{Y}}_1^\pi$ . Then, we evaluate both the training error  $\| \mathcal{Y}_1^\pi - \hat{\mathcal{Y}}_1^\pi \|_{\mathrm{F}} / \sqrt{100 \cdot 50 \cdot 4}$  and the prediction error  $\| \mathcal{Y}_2^\pi - \hat{\mathcal{Y}}_1^\pi \|_{\mathrm{F}} / \sqrt{100 \cdot 50 \cdot 4}$  for each  $\pi$  and provide both the average error and standard deviation in Table 3. As we can see, although the proposed method yields a larger training error, there is a significant advantage in the prediction error to the classic Gaussian-likelihood-based methods (HOSVD or HOOI). This data analysis also illustrates the merit of the generalized tensor estimation framework for handling non-Gaussian tensor data.

# APPENDIX C: PROOF OF THEOREM 3.1

In this section, we provide the proof of technical results on error contraction. We divide the proof into five steps. In Step 1, we introduce the notations and conditions that are used to develop the theory. Then in Steps 2, 3, 4, we prove the one-step error contraction and provide the convergence analysis. Finally in Step 5, we verify the conditions imposed in Step 1.

Step 1 (Notations and Conditions) To simplify the rest of the proof, we assume  $b = \overline{\lambda}^{1/4}$  holds, since the following argument also holds by changing the absolute constants when we are under the condition that  $c\overline{\lambda}^{1/4} \leq b \leq C\overline{\lambda}^{1/4}$  for fixed  $c$  and  $C$ . We first introduce or rephrase the following list of notations. Let  $\mathcal{X}^*$  be the target low-rank tensor that satisfies  $\mathcal{X}^* = [[S^*; \mathbf{U}_1^*, \mathbf{U}_2^*, \mathbf{U}_3^*]]$  such that  $\mathbf{U}_k^{*^\top}\mathbf{U}_k^* = b^2\mathbf{I}_{r_k}$ ,  $\mathbf{U}_k^* \in \mathcal{C}_k$  for  $k = 1, 2, 3$ . For each step  $t = 0, 1, \ldots, t_{max}$ , we define

$(N_{1})$  Error measurement

$$
E^{(t)} = \min_{\substack{\mathbf{R}_{k}\in \mathbb{O}_{p_{k},r_{k}}\\ k = 1,2,3}}\left\{\sum_{k = 1}^{3}\left\| \mathbf{U}_{k}^{(t)} - \mathbf{U}_{k}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2} + \left\| \mathcal{S}^{(t)} - \llbracket \mathcal{S}^{*};\mathbf{R}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{R}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\top}\rrbracket \right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\right\}
$$

$$
(\mathbf{R}_{1}^{(t)},\mathbf{R}_{2}^{(t)},\mathbf{R}_{3}^{(t)}) = \operatorname *{arg  min}_{\substack{\mathbf{R}_{k}\in \mathbb{O}_{p_{k},r_{k}}\\ k = 1,2,3}}\left\{\sum_{k = 1}^{3}\left\| \mathbf{U}_{k}^{(t)} - \mathbf{U}_{k}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2} + \left\| \mathcal{S}^{(t)} - \llbracket \mathcal{S}^{*};\mathbf{R}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{R}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\top}\rrbracket \right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\right\}
$$

$$
(\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{1}^{(t)},\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{2}^{(t)},\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{3}^{(t)}) = \operatorname *{arg  min}_{\substack{\mathbf{R}_{k}\in \mathbb{O}_{p_{k},r_{k}}\\ k = 1,2,3}}\left\{\sum_{k = 1}^{3}\left\| \tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{k}^{(t)} - \mathbf{U}_{k}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2} + \left\| \tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{(t)} - \llbracket \mathcal{S}^{*};\mathbf{R}_{1}^{\top},\mathbf{R}_{2}^{\top},\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\top}\rrbracket \right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\right\}
$$

$(N_{2})$  Dual loadings

$$
\mathbf {V} _ {k} ^ {(t)} = \left(\mathbf {U} _ {k + 2} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {k + 1} ^ {(t)}\right) \left(\mathcal {M} _ {k} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)})\right) ^ {\top}, \quad k = 1, 2, 3
$$

$(N_{3})$  Signal tensors

$$
\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} = [   [ \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}; \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} ]   ]
$$

$$
\mathcal {X} _ {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t)} = \llbracket \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \rrbracket
$$

$$
\mathcal {X} _ {k} ^ {(t)} = \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} \times_ {k} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \times_ {k + 1} \mathbf {U} _ {k + 1} ^ {(t)} \times_ {k + 2} \mathbf {U} _ {k + 2} ^ {(t)}, \quad k = 1, 2, 3
$$

We also assume the following conditions hold: (They are verified in Step 5)

$(A_{1})$  For any  $t = 0,1,\ldots ,t_{max}$  , we have

$$
\left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| \leq 1. 0 1 b, \quad \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {k} \left(\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}\right) \right\| \leq \frac {1 . 0 1 \bar {\lambda}}{b ^ {3}}, \quad k = 1, 2, 3. \tag {C.3}
$$

$(A_{2})$  For  $c_{0} = \frac{1}{20000}$  and any  $t = 0,1,\ldots ,t_{max}$ , we have

$$
\mathrm {(C . 4)} \qquad E ^ {(t)} \le c _ {0} \frac {\alpha \beta \sqrt {\lambda}}{\kappa^ {3 / 2}}.
$$

Note that by RCG-condition (3.1), one has

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha \| \mathcal {X} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{4 \alpha} \| \nabla f (\mathcal {X}) - \nabla f (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \geq \| \mathcal {X} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \cdot \| \nabla f (\mathcal {X}) - f (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \geq \alpha \| \mathcal {X} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \beta \| \nabla f (\mathcal {X}) - \nabla f (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

and thus  $\alpha \beta \leq 1 / 4$ . Therefore, (C.4) further implies

$$
E ^ {(t)} \leq \frac {c _ {0} \sqrt {\underline {{\lambda}}}}{4 \kappa^ {3 / 2}}.
$$

Step 2 (Descent of  $E^{(t)}$ ) In this step, we show that under conditions (C.3) and (C.4),

$$
E ^ {(t + 1)} \leq E ^ {(t)} - 2 \eta \left(Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 1} + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} Q _ {k, 1}\right) + \eta^ {2} \left(Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 2} + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} Q _ {k, 2}\right), \tag {C.5}
$$

where

(C.6)

$$
Q _ {k, 1} = \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} _ {k} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle + \frac {a}{4} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {0} a \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {1 / 2}}{1 6} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2},
$$

$$
Q _ {k, 2} = 8 \overline {{\lambda}} ^ {2} b ^ {- 2} \left(\xi^ {2} + \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right) + \frac {5}{2} a ^ {2} b ^ {2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2},
$$

$$
Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 1} = \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} _ {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle ,
$$

$$
Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 2} = 4 b ^ {6} \left(\xi^ {2} + \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right).
$$

By definition of  $E^{(t + 1)}$ , we first have

(C.7)

$$
\begin{array}{l} E ^ {(t + 1)} = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t + 1)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \left\| \mathcal {S} ^ {(t + 1)} - [ \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t + 1) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t + 1) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t + 1) \top} ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \stackrel {(a)} {\leq} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \tilde {\mathbf {R}} _ {k} ^ {(t + 1)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \left\| \mathcal {S} ^ {(t + 1)} - [   [ \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \tilde {\mathbf {R}} _ {1} ^ {(t + 1) \top}, \tilde {\mathbf {R}} _ {2} ^ {(t + 1) \top}, \tilde {\mathbf {R}} _ {3} ^ {(t + 1) \top} ]   ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \stackrel {(b)} {\leq} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \tilde {\mathbf {U}} _ {k} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \tilde {\mathbf {R}} _ {k} ^ {(t + 1)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \left\| \tilde {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t + 1)} - [   [ \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \tilde {\mathbf {R}} _ {1} ^ {(t + 1) \top}, \tilde {\mathbf {R}} _ {2} ^ {(t + 1) \top}, \tilde {\mathbf {R}} _ {3} ^ {(t + 1) \top} ]   ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \stackrel {(c)} {\leq} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \tilde {\mathbf {U}} _ {k} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \left\| \tilde {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t + 1)} - [ [ \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ] ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Here, (a) and (c) comes from the definitions of  $(\mathbf{R}_1^{(t + 1)},\mathbf{R}_2^{(t + 1)},\mathbf{R}_3^{(t + 1)})$  and  $(\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_1^{(t + 1)},\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_2^{(t + 1)},\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_3^{(t + 1)})$ , while (b) comes from the projection step  $\mathbf{U}_k^{(t + 1)} = \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C}_k}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_k^{(t + 1)}),\mathcal{S}^{(t + 1)} = \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{C}_S}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{(t + 1)})$  as  $\mathcal{C}_k$  and  $\mathcal{C}_S$  are convex and rotation invariant sets.

Now we analyze the error on loadings and core tensor separately. Specifically, we will show:

$$
\mathrm {(C . 8)} \quad \left\| \tilde {\mathbf {U}} _ {k} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - 2 \eta Q _ {k, 1} + \eta^ {2} Q _ {k, 2}, \quad k = 1, 2, 3.
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {S} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathcal {S} ^ {*} \times_ {1} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {2} \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {3} \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \tag {C.9} \\ \leq \left\| \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {S} ^ {*} \times_ {1} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {2} \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {3} \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - 2 \eta Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 1} + \eta^ {2} Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Then (C.5) can be obtained by combining (C.7), (C.10) and (C.9).

- To show (C.8), we first focus on  $\left\| \tilde{\mathbf{U}}_1^{(t + 1)} - \mathbf{U}_1^*\mathbf{R}_1^{(t)}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^2$ . By plugging in the gradient (Lemma 2.1), we have the following decomposition.

(C.10)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \tilde {\mathbf {U}} _ {1} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ = \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \eta \left(\mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} + a \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} (\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I})\right) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ = \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \eta^ {2} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} + a \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} (\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ - 2 \eta \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\rangle \\ - 2 \eta a \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \left(\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I}\right) \right\rangle \\ \end{array}
$$

We bound the last three terms separately. First, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} + a \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} (\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \leq 2 \left(\left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + a ^ {2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} (\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

The first term can be bounded as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \leq 2 \left(\left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*})) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} \left(\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*})\right) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right) \\ = 2 \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} \left(\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*})\right) \left(\mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)}\right) \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + 2 \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} \left(\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*})\right) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Notice that by the duality of Frobenius norm, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*})) \left(\mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)}\right) \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) ^ {\top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ = \sup  _ {\mathbf {W} _ {1} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p _ {1} \times r _ {1}}, \| \mathbf {W} _ {1} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq 1} \left\langle \mathcal {M} _ {1} \left(\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*})\right) \left(\mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)}\right) \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) ^ {\top}, \mathbf {W} _ {1} \right\rangle \\ = \sup  _ {\mathbf {W} _ {1} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p _ {1} \times r _ {1}}, \| \mathbf {W} _ {1} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq 1} \left\langle \mathcal {M} _ {1} \left(\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*})\right), \mathbf {W} _ {1} \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) \left(\mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)}\right) ^ {\top} \right\rangle \\ = \sup  _ {\mathbf {W} _ {1} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p _ {1} \times r _ {1}}, \| \mathbf {W} _ {1} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq 1} \left\langle \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}), \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} \times_ {1} \mathbf {W} _ {1} \times_ {2} \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \times_ {3} \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \right\rangle \\ \leq \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) \right\| \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \right\| \cdot \xi , \\ \end{array}
$$

then it follows that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq 2 \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \xi^ {2} \\ + 2 \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \right\| ^ {2} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) \right\| ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {(\mathrm {C . 3})} {\leq} 2 \cdot (1. 0 1 b) ^ {4} \cdot \frac {(1 . 0 1 \overline {{\lambda}}) ^ {2}}{b ^ {6}} \xi^ {2} + 2 \cdot (1. 0 1 b) ^ {4} \cdot \frac {(1 . 0 1 \overline {{\lambda}}) ^ {2}}{b ^ {6}} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \leq 4 \overline {{\lambda}} ^ {2} b ^ {- 2} \left(\xi^ {2} + \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

In addition,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \left(\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}}\right) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \stackrel {(\mathrm {C . 3})} {\leq} (1. 0 1 b) ^ {2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq \frac {5}{4} b ^ {2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Combining the two inequalities above, we have

(C.11)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} + a \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} (\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \leq 8 \overline {{\lambda}} ^ {2} b ^ {- 2} \left(\xi_ {1} ^ {2} + \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right) + \frac {5}{2} a ^ {2} b ^ {2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} = Q _ {1, 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

For the third term on the right hand side of (C.10), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left<   \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathcal {M} _ {1} \left(\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})\right) \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right> \\ = \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {V} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathcal {M} _ {1} \left(\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})\right) \right\rangle \\ \end{array}
$$

(C.12)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {(\mathbf {C}. 1)} {=} \left\langle \boldsymbol {\mathcal {X}} ^ {(t)} - \boldsymbol {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t)} \times_ {1} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \times_ {2} \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \times_ {3} \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\boldsymbol {\mathcal {X}} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle \\ \stackrel {(\mathrm {C . 2})} {=} \left<   \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right > . \\ \end{array}
$$

For the last term on the right hand side of (C.10), we have

(C.13)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} (\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}}) \right\rangle \\ = \left\langle {\bf {U}} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} {\bf {U}} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - {\bf {U}} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} {\bf {U}} _ {1} ^ {*} {\bf {R}} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, {\bf {U}} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} {\bf {U}} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} {\bf {I}} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle \\ = \frac {1}{2} \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {* \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*}, \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle \\ + \frac {1}{2} \left\langle {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {* \top} {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {*} - 2 {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {*} {\bf R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} + {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} {\bf I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle \\ \stackrel {(a)} {=} \frac {1}{2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2} \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \left(\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}\right), \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle \\ + \frac {1}{2} \left\langle {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {* \top} {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {*} - {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {*} {\bf R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} {\bf U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} {\bf I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle . \\ \end{array}
$$

Here, (a) is due to the assumption that  $\mathbf{U}_1^{*^\top}\mathbf{U}_1^* = b^2\mathbf{I}_{r_1}$ . Since

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {* \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle \\ \stackrel {(b)} {=} \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {* \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} - \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {* \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle \\ \stackrel {(c)} {=} \left\langle \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {* \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {* \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle \\ = \left\langle \left(\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}\right) ^ {\top} \left(\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)}\right), \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle , \\ \end{array}
$$

where (b) is due to the fact that  $\langle \mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\rangle = \langle \mathbf{A}^{\top},\mathbf{B}\rangle$  for symmetric matrix  $\mathbf{B}$  and (c) holds because  $\mathbf{U}_1^{*^\top}\mathbf{U}_1^* = b^2\mathbf{I}_{r_1}$  and  $\mathbf{R}_1^\top \mathbf{R}_1 = \mathbf{I}_{r_1}$ , we further have

(C.14)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} (\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}}) \right\rangle \\ = \frac {1}{2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ + \frac {1}{2} \left\langle \left(\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)}\right) ^ {\top} \left(\mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)}\right), \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\rangle \\ \geq \frac {1}{2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \frac {1}{2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ \geq \frac {1}{2} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \left(\frac {1}{4} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{4} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {4}\right) \\ \geq \frac {1}{4} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \frac {1}{4} E ^ {(t)} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \geq \frac {1}{4} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {1}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {0}}{1 6} \sqrt {\underline {{\lambda}}} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Here, the last inequality of (C.14) comes from the definition of  $E^{(t)}$ , (C.4),  $\kappa \geq 1$ , and

$$
\alpha \beta \leq 1 / 4: \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq E ^ {(t)} \leq c _ {0} \frac {\alpha \beta \sqrt {\lambda}}{\kappa^ {3 / 2}} \leq \frac {c _ {0}}{4} \sqrt {\lambda}.
$$

Combining (C.10), (C.11), (C.12), and (C.14), we obtain

$$
\left\| \tilde {\mathbf {U}} _ {1} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - 2 \eta Q _ {1, 1} + \eta^ {2} Q _ {1, 2}.
$$

Here,  $Q_{1,1}, Q_{1,2}$  are defined in (C.6). Then more generally,

$$
\left\| \tilde {\mathbf {U}} _ {k} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - 2 \eta Q _ {k, 1} + \eta^ {2} Q _ {k, 2}, \quad k = 1, 2, 3.
$$

This finishes the proof of (C.8).

- Next we prove (C.9). Specifically, we have the following decomposition,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \tilde {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t + 1)} - [   [ \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ]   ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ = \left\| \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} - [   [ \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ]   ] - \eta [   [ \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}); \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ]   ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ = \left\| \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} - [   [ \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ]   ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \eta^ {2} \left\| [   [ \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}); \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ]   ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ - 2 \eta \left\langle \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} - [   [ \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ] ], [   [ \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}); \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ] ] \right\rangle . \\ \end{array}
$$

On the one hand, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} - \llbracket \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \rrbracket , \llbracket \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}); \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \rrbracket \right\rangle \\ = \left\langle \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) - \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {*}) \left(\mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t)}\right), \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathcal {M} _ {1} \left(\nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)})\right) \left(\mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)}\right) \right\rangle \\ = \left\langle \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) \left(\mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)}\right) ^ {\top} - \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathcal {M} _ {1} (\mathcal {S} ^ {*}) \left(\mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \otimes \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}\right), \right. \\ \left. \mathcal {M} _ {1} \left(\nabla L \left(\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}\right)\right) \right\rangle \\ = \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {S} ^ {*} \times_ {1} \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {2} \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {3} \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle \\ = \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} _ {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle = Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 1}. \\ \end{array}
$$

On the other hand, by noticing that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \bigg\|\llbracket \nabla L(\mathcal{X}^{*});\mathbf{U}_{1}^{(t)\top},\mathbf{U}_{2}^{(t)\top},\mathbf{U}_{3}^{(t)\top}\rrbracket \bigg\|_{F} = \sup_{\substack{\mathcal{S}\in \mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}\times r_{3}}\\ \| \mathcal{S}\|_{F}\leq 1}}\Big\langle \llbracket \nabla L(\mathcal{X}^{*});\mathbf{U}_{1}^{(t)\top},\mathbf{U}_{2}^{(t)\top},\mathbf{U}_{3}^{(t)\top}\rrbracket ,\mathcal{S}\Big\rangle  \\ = \sup_{\substack{\mathcal{S}\in \mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}\times r_{3}}\\ \| \mathcal{S}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq 1}}\left\langle \nabla L(\mathcal{X}^{*}),[[\mathcal{S};\mathbf{U}_{1}^{(t)},\mathbf{U}_{2}^{(t)},\mathbf{U}_{3}^{(t)}]]\right\rangle \\ \leq \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \right\| \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \right\| \cdot \xi , \\ \end{array}
$$

we also have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \llbracket \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}); \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ] \rrbracket_ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \right. \\ \leq 2 \left\| \llbracket \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}); \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \rrbracket \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ + 2 \left\| [   [ \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}); \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} ]   ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \leq 2 \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \xi^ {2} + \\ + 2 \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\stackrel {\text {(C . 3)}} {\leq} 4 b ^ {6} \left(\xi^ {2} + \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right) = Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 2}.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {S} ^ {(t + 1)} - \mathcal {S} ^ {*} \times_ {1} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {2} \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {3} \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t)} - \boldsymbol {\mathcal {S}} ^ {*} \times_ {1} \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {2} \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top} \times_ {3} \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - 2 \eta Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 1} + \eta^ {2} Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

which proves (C.9).

Step 3 In this Step 3, we aim to develop a lower bound for  $Q_{S,1} + \sum_{k=1}^{3} Q_{k,1}$ . By definitions of  $Q_{S,1}$  and  $Q_{k,1}$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 1} + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} Q _ {k, 1} \\ = \left\langle 4 \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} _ {S} ^ {(t)} - \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \mathcal {X} _ {k} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle \\ + a \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left(\frac {1}{4} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {0}}{1 6} \sqrt {\underline {{\lambda}}} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} = \left\langle 4 \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \left(3 \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} + \mathcal {X} ^ {*} - \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon}\right), \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle \tag {C.15} \\ + a \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left(\frac {1}{4} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {0}}{1 6} \sqrt {\underline {{\lambda}}} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right) \\ = \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} + \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle \\ + a \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left(\frac {1}{4} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \frac {c _ {0}}{1 6} \sqrt {\underline {{\lambda}}} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

Here,  $\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}$  is a tensor of small amplitude that is obtained by applying Lemma E.3. By (C.3), the quantities  $B_{1}, B_{2}, B_{3}$  in the context of Lemma E.3 satisfy  $B_{1} \leq 1.01b$ ,  $B_{2} \leq \frac{1.01\overline{\lambda}}{b^{3}}$  and  $B_{3} \leq E^{(t)}$ . Thus, Lemma E.3 implies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq B _ {3} ^ {3 / 2} B _ {2} + 3 B _ {1} B _ {2} B _ {3} + 3 B _ {3} B _ {1} ^ {2} \\ \leq 1. 0 1 \bar {\lambda} b ^ {- 3} (E ^ {(t)}) ^ {3 / 2} + 3 (1. 0 1 b) ^ {2} E ^ {(t)} + 3 (1. 0 1 b) ^ {2} E ^ {(t)} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leq \left(1. 0 1 c _ {0} ^ {1 / 2} \bar {\lambda} \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {1 / 4} b ^ {- 3} + 6 \cdot 1. 0 1 ^ {2} b ^ {2}\right) E ^ {(t)} \tag {C.16} \\ = \left(1. 0 1 c _ {0} ^ {1 / 2} \kappa^ {1 / 4} + 6 \cdot 1. 0 1 ^ {2} \kappa^ {1 / 2}\right) \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {1 / 2} E ^ {(t)} \\ \leq 6. 5 \kappa^ {1 / 2} \lambda^ {1 / 2} E ^ {(t)}. \\ \end{array}
$$

The first term on the right hand side of (C.15) can be further bounded as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} + \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle = \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle \\ + \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} + \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle + \left\langle \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {(e)} {\geq} \alpha \left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \beta \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \| \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ - \left| \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} + \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle \right|. \\ \end{array}
$$

Here  $(e)$  is due to RCG condition and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Now we have

$$
\| \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq \frac {\beta}{2} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \beta} \| \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}
$$

$$
\stackrel {(\mathrm {C}. 1 6)} {\leq} \frac {\beta}{2} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \beta} \left(6. 5 \kappa^ {1 / 2} \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {1 / 2} E ^ {(t)}\right) ^ {2}
$$

$$
\stackrel {(\mathrm {C . 4})} {\leq} \frac {\beta}{2} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + 2 4 c _ {0} \frac {\alpha \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {3 / 2}}{\kappa^ {1 / 2}} E ^ {(t)},
$$

and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} + \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle \right| \\ = \left| \left\langle 4 \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} _ {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t)} - \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \mathcal {X} _ {k} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle \right| \\ \leq \left| \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} _ {\mathcal {S}} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle \right| + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left| \left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} _ {k} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle \right| \\ = \left| \left\langle \llbracket \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} - \llbracket \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \rrbracket ; \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \rrbracket , \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle \right| \\ + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left| \left\langle \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} \times_ {k} (\mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)}) \times_ {k + 1} \mathbf {U} _ {k + 1} ^ {(t)} \times_ {k + 2} \mathbf {U} _ {k + 2} ^ {(t)}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\rangle \right| \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leq \xi \left\| \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} - \llbracket \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t) \top}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t) \top} \rrbracket \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \right\| \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \right\| \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \right\| \\ + \xi \cdot \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {k} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) \right\| \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k + 2} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {U} _ {k + 1} ^ {(t)} \right\| \\ \leq \left(\| \mathbf {U} _ {1} ^ {(t)} \| \| \mathbf {U} _ {2} ^ {(t)} \| \| \mathbf {U} _ {3} ^ {(t)} \| + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {k} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) \right\| \| \mathbf {U} _ {k + 2} ^ {(t)} \| \| \mathbf {U} _ {k + 1} ^ {(t)} \right\|) \xi (E ^ {(t)}) ^ {1 / 2} \\ \stackrel {(C. 3)} {\leq} \left(1. 0 1 ^ {3} b ^ {3} + 3 \cdot 1. 0 1 ^ {3} \bar {\lambda} b ^ {- 3} b ^ {2}\right) \xi (E ^ {(t)}) ^ {1 / 2} \\ \leq \frac {9}{2} \left(b ^ {3} + \bar {\lambda} b ^ {- 1}\right) \xi \sqrt {E ^ {(t)}} \leq \frac {c _ {1}}{4} \frac {\alpha (b ^ {3} + \bar {\lambda} b ^ {- 1}) ^ {2}}{\kappa^ {2}} E ^ {(t)} + \frac {8 1 \kappa^ {2}}{4 c _ {1} \alpha} \xi^ {2} \\ = c _ {1} \frac {\alpha \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {3 / 2}}{\kappa^ {1 / 2}} E ^ {(t)} + \frac {8 1 \kappa^ {2}}{4 c _ {1} \alpha} \xi^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Here,  $c_{1}$  is some small constant that can be specified as  $c_{1} = 1 / 3000$ . Then it follows from the previous three inequalities that

$$
\left\langle \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} + \mathcal {H} _ {\varepsilon}, \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) \right\rangle
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \geq \alpha \left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {\beta}{2} \left\| \nabla L \left(\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}\right) - \nabla L \left(\mathcal {X} ^ {*}\right) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \tag {C.18} \\ - \left(2 4 c _ {0} + c _ {1}\right) \frac {\alpha \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {3 / 2}}{\kappa^ {1 / 2}} E ^ {(t)} - \frac {8 1 \kappa^ {2}}{4 c _ {1} \alpha} \xi^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Now applying Lemma E.2 with  $c_{d} = 0.01$  and  $b = \overline{\lambda}$ , we have

$$
E ^ {(t)} \leq 4 8 0 \kappa^ {2} b ^ {- 6} \left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + 8 0 b ^ {- 2} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}. \tag {C.19}
$$

By setting  $a = \frac{4\alpha b^4}{3\kappa^2} = \frac{4\alpha\lambda}{3\kappa}$  and applying (C.15), (C.18) and (C.19), we have (C.20)

$$
\begin{array}{l} Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 1} + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} Q _ {k, 1} \geq \alpha \left(\left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {\kappa^ {- 2} b ^ {4}}{6} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right) \\ + \frac {\beta}{2} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {a}{8} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ - \frac {c _ {0}}{1 2} \frac {\alpha \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {3 / 2}}{\kappa} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - (2 4 c _ {0} + c _ {1}) \frac {\alpha \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {3 / 2}}{\kappa^ {1 / 2}} E ^ {(t)} - \frac {8 1 \kappa^ {2}}{4 c _ {1} \alpha} \xi^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \stackrel {(\mathbf {C}. 1 9)} {\geq} \frac {\alpha \kappa^ {3 / 2} \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {3 / 2}}{4 8 0 \kappa^ {2}} E ^ {(t)} + \frac {\beta}{2} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {a}{8} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ - \left(2 4 c _ {0} + c _ {1}\right) \frac {\alpha \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {3 / 2}}{\kappa^ {1 / 2}} E ^ {(t)} - \frac {8 1 \kappa^ {2}}{4 c _ {1} \alpha} \xi^ {2} \\ \geq \frac {\alpha \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {3 / 2}}{\kappa^ {1 / 2}} \left(\frac {1}{4 8 0} - 2 4 c _ {0} - c _ {1}\right) E ^ {(t)} + \frac {\beta}{2} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - \frac {8 1 \kappa^ {2}}{4 c _ {1} \alpha} \xi^ {2} \\ + \frac {a}{8} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Finally we can plug in  $c_0 = \frac{1}{20000}$  and  $c_1 = \frac{1}{3000}$  and establish a lower bound for  $Q_{S,1} + \sum_{k=1}^{3} Q_{k,1}$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 1} + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} Q _ {k, 1} \geq \rho \frac {\alpha \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {3 / 2}}{\kappa^ {1 / 2}} E ^ {(t)} + \frac {\beta}{2} \left\| \nabla L \left(\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}\right) - \nabla L \left(\mathcal {X} ^ {*}\right) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \tag {C.21} \\ + \frac {a}{8} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} - C _ {1} \frac {\kappa^ {2}}{\alpha} \xi^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\rho = 1 / 2000$  and  $C_1$  is some universal constant.

Step 4 (Convergence Analysis) In this step we combine all results in previous steps to establish the error bound for  $E^{(t)}$  and  $\| \mathcal{X}^{(t)} - \mathcal{X}^{*}\|_{\mathrm{F}}$ . By plugging in  $a = \frac{4\alpha b^4}{3\kappa^2}$  to the definitions of  $Q_{S,2}$  and  $Q_{k,2}$  in (C.6), we have

(C.22)

$$
\begin{array}{l} Q _ {\mathcal {S}, 2} + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} Q _ {k, 2} \\ \leq 2 8 b ^ {6} \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + \frac {5 \alpha^ {2} b ^ {1 0}}{\kappa^ {4}} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} + 2 8 b ^ {6} \xi^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Combining (C.7), (C.21), and (C.22) and setting  $\eta = \eta_0\beta /b^6$ , for any positive constant  $\eta_0\leq \frac{1}{28}$ , we have:

$$
\begin{array}{l} E ^ {(t + 1)} \leq E ^ {(t)} - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}} E ^ {(t)} - \left(\frac {\beta^ {2}}{b ^ {6}} \eta_ {0} - \frac {2 8 \beta^ {2}}{b ^ {6}} \eta_ {0} ^ {2}\right) \left\| \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {(t)}) - \nabla L (\mathcal {X} ^ {*}) \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ - \left(\frac {\alpha \beta b ^ {- 2}}{3 \kappa^ {2}} \eta_ {0} - \frac {5 \alpha^ {2} \beta^ {2} b ^ {- 2}}{\kappa^ {4}} \eta_ {0} ^ {2}\right) \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - b ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {r _ {k}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \\ + \frac {2 C _ {1} \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \eta_ {0} + 2 8 2 \beta^ {2} b ^ {- 6} \xi^ {2} \eta_ {0} ^ {2}, \\ \frac {\beta^ {2}}{b ^ {6}} \eta_ {0} - \frac {2 8 \beta^ {2}}{b ^ {6}} \eta_ {0} ^ {2} = \frac {\beta^ {2} \eta_ {0}}{b ^ {6}} (1 - 2 8 \eta_ {0}) > 0, \\ \frac {\alpha \beta b ^ {- 2}}{3 \kappa^ {2}} \eta_ {0} - \frac {5 \alpha^ {2} \beta^ {2} b ^ {- 2}}{\kappa^ {4}} \eta_ {0} ^ {2} = \frac {\alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2} b ^ {2}} \left(\frac {1}{3} - \frac {5 \alpha \beta}{\kappa^ {2}} \eta_ {0}\right) ^ {(f)} > 0. \\ \end{array}
$$

$(f)$  is also due to  $\beta \leq \frac{1}{4\alpha}$  and  $\kappa \geq 1$ . Then it follows that

$$
E ^ {(t + 1)} \leq \left(1 - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) E ^ {(t)} + \frac {2 C _ {1} \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \eta_ {0} + 2 8 \beta^ {2} b ^ {- 6} \xi^ {2} \eta_ {0} ^ {2}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leq \left(1 - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) E ^ {(t)} + \frac {2 C _ {1} \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \eta_ {0} + \frac {3 \beta b ^ {- 6} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \eta_ {0} ^ {2} \tag {C.23} \\ \leq \left(1 - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) E ^ {(t)} + \frac {(2 C _ {1} + 1) \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \eta_ {0} \\ \end{array}
$$

for  $t = 0,1,\ldots ,t_{max}$  . Now we use induction to show

$$
E ^ {(t)} \leq \frac {\left(2 C _ {1} + 1\right) \kappa^ {4}}{2 \rho \alpha^ {2} b ^ {6}} \xi^ {2} + \left(1 - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) ^ {t} E ^ {(0)}. \tag {C.24}
$$

When  $t = 0$ , (C.24) clearly holds. We assume it holds at  $t = t_0$ , then for  $t = t_0 + 1$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} E ^ {(t _ {0} + 1)} \stackrel {\mathrm {(C . 2 3)}} {\leq} \left(1 - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) E ^ {(t _ {0})} + \frac {(2 C _ {1} + 1) \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \eta_ {0} \\ \stackrel {(C. 2 4)} {\leq} \left(1 - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) ^ {t _ {0} + 1} E ^ {(0)} + \left(1 - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) \frac {(2 C _ {1} + 1) \kappa^ {4}}{2 \rho \alpha^ {2} b ^ {6}} \xi^ {2} \\ + \frac {(2 C _ {1} + 1) \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \eta_ {0} \\ = \left(1 - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) ^ {t _ {0} + 1} E ^ {(0)} + \frac {(2 C _ {1} + 1) \kappa^ {4}}{2 \rho \alpha^ {2} b ^ {6}} \xi^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

thus (C.24) also holds at step  $t_0 + 1$ . By induction, (C.24) holds for any  $t = 0, 1, \ldots, t_{\max}$ . Then we can apply Lemma E.2 again and obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(t)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq 4 2 b ^ {6} E ^ {(t)} \leq \frac {4 2 (2 C _ {1} + 1) \kappa^ {4}}{\rho \alpha^ {2}} \xi^ {2} + 4 2 b ^ {6} \left(1 - \frac {\rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) ^ {t} E ^ {(0)} \\ \leq C \left(\frac {\kappa^ {4}}{\alpha^ {2}} \xi^ {2} + \kappa^ {2} \left(1 - \frac {2 \rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) ^ {t} \left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(0)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $C$  is some universal constant.

Step 5 In this step, we show that conditions (C.3) and (C.4) hold. We first apply induction on (C.23) to prove (C.4). Since  $\| \mathbf{U}_k^*\top \mathbf{U}_k^* - b^2\mathbf{I}_{r_k}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^2 = 0$ , by Lemma E.2 and the initialization error bound, we have

$$
E ^ {(0)} \leq 4 8 0 b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \left\| \mathcal {X} ^ {(0)} - \mathcal {X} ^ {*} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} ^ {2} \leq c _ {0} \frac {\alpha \beta \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {1 / 2}}{\kappa^ {3 / 2}}.
$$

Now assume (C.4) holds at step  $t$ , then for step  $t + 1$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} E ^ {(t + 1)} \stackrel {\mathrm {(C . 2 3)}} {\leq} \left(1 - \frac {\rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) E ^ {(t)} + \frac {(2 C _ {1} + 1) \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \eta_ {0} \\ \leq \left(1 - \frac {\rho \alpha \beta \eta_ {0}}{\kappa^ {2}}\right) \frac {c _ {0} \alpha \beta \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {1 / 2}}{\kappa^ {3 / 2}} + \frac {(2 C _ {1} + 1) \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \eta_ {0} \\ \leq c _ {0} \frac {\alpha \beta \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {1 / 2}}{\kappa^ {3 / 2}} - \eta_ {0} \left(\rho c _ {0} \frac {\alpha^ {2} \beta^ {2} \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {1 / 2}}{\kappa^ {7 / 2}} - \frac {(2 C _ {1} + 1) \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

By the signal-noise-ratio assumption that  $\underline{\lambda}^2 \geq C \frac{\kappa^4}{\alpha^3 \beta} \xi^2$  for some universal big constant  $C$ , we know

$$
\rho c _ {0} \frac {\alpha^ {2} \beta^ {2} \underline {{\lambda}} ^ {1 / 2}}{\kappa^ {7 / 2}} - \frac {(2 C _ {1} + 1) \beta b ^ {- 6} \kappa^ {2} \xi^ {2}}{\alpha} \geq 0
$$

and then  $E^{(t + 1)} \leq c_0 \frac{\alpha \beta \lambda^{1/2}}{\kappa^{3/2}}$ . By induction, (C.4) holds for any  $t = 0, 1, \ldots, t_{\max}$ .

Then we use (C.4) to prove (C.3). Note that in Step  $t$ , we have  $E^{(t)} \leq c_0 \frac{\alpha \beta \lambda^{1/2}}{\kappa^{3/2}} = c_0 \frac{\alpha \beta b^2}{\kappa^2} \leq \frac{c_0}{4} b^2$ , which implies that for any  $k = 1, 2, 3$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| \leq \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| + \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| \\ = b + \left\| \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {U} _ {k} ^ {*} \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq (1 + \frac {\sqrt {c _ {0}}}{2}) b \leq 1. 0 1 b, \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathcal {M} _ {k} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) \right\| \leq \left\| \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t) \top} \mathcal {M} _ {k} (\mathcal {S} ^ {*}) (\mathbf {R} _ {3} \otimes \mathbf {R} _ {2}) \right\| \\ + \left\| \mathbf {R} _ {k} ^ {(t) \top} \mathcal {M} _ {k} (\mathcal {S} ^ {*}) \left(\mathbf {R} _ {k + 1} ^ {(t)} \otimes \mathbf {R} _ {k + 2} ^ {(t)}\right) - \mathcal {M} _ {k} (\mathcal {S} ^ {(t)}) \right\| \\ \leq \overline {{\lambda}} / b ^ {3} + \left\| \mathcal {S} ^ {(t)} - [   [ \mathcal {S} ^ {*}; \mathbf {R} _ {1} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {R} _ {2} ^ {(t)}, \mathbf {R} _ {3} ^ {(t)} ]   ] \right\| _ {\mathrm {F}} \\ \leq \bar {\lambda} / b ^ {3} + \frac {\sqrt {c _ {0}}}{2} b \leq 1. 0 1 \bar {\lambda} / b ^ {3}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Now we have finished the proof of Theorem 3.1.

# APPENDIX D: PROOFS OF OTHER THEOREMS

We collect the proofs of all the other theorems in this section.

D.1. Proof of Theorem 4.1. Without loss of generality, we assume  $\sigma = 1$ . Let  $\mathcal{X}^{*} = [[S,\mathbf{U}_{1},\mathbf{U}_{2},\mathbf{U}_{3}]]$ , where  $\mathbf{U}_1,\mathbf{U}_2,\mathbf{U}_3$  are orthogonal matrices, and let  $\mathbf{U}_k^* = b\mathbf{U}_k$  for  $k = 1,2,3$  and  $S^{*} = S / b^{3}$ . Also recall that  $\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_k = \mathrm{SVD}_{r_k}(\mathcal{M}_k(\mathcal{V}))$ , and  $\mathbf{U}_k^{(0)} = b\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_k^{(0)}$ . We would like to apply Theorem 3.1 to obtain the result. In the context of sub-Gaussian tensor PCA, we have

$$
\nabla L \left(\mathcal {X} ^ {*}\right) = \mathcal {X} - \mathcal {Y} =: \mathcal {Z},
$$

where all entries of  $\mathbf{Z}$  are independent mean-zero sub-Gaussian random variables such that

$$
\| \mathcal{Z}_{ijk}\|_{\psi_{2}} = \sup_{q\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left(|\mathcal{Z}_{ijk}|^{q}\right)^{1 / q} / q^{1 / 2}\leq 1.
$$

We claim that with probability at least  $1 - C \exp(-\underline{c}p)$ , the following conditions hold:

$$
\left\| \sin \Theta (\tilde {\mathbf {U}} _ {k}, \mathbf {U} _ {k}) \right\| \leq \frac {\sqrt {p _ {k}} \underline {{\lambda}} + \sqrt {p _ {1} p _ {2} p _ {3}}}{\underline {{\lambda}} ^ {2}}, \quad k = 1, 2, 3,
$$

$$
\xi = \sup  _ {\substack {\mathcal {T} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p _ {1} \times p _ {2} \times p _ {3}}, \\ \operatorname {rank} (\mathcal {T}) \leq (r _ {1}, r _ {2}, r _ {3}), \\ \| \mathcal {T} \| _ {\mathrm {F}} \leq 1}} \langle \mathcal {Z}, \mathcal {T} \rangle \leq C \left(\sqrt {r _ {1} r _ {2} r _ {3}} + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {3} \sqrt {p _ {k} r _ {k}}\right). \tag{D.1}
$$
